/Users/havarddaleng/.claude/projects/-Users-havarddaleng/memory/no-planning-vocabulary-in-code.md:11:Nothing shipped into a repo may reference the private planning apparatus in `_workflow/`: no `FR-3`, no `spec-004`, no `004-A1`, no `_workflow/...` paths, no "as required by the spec". Applies to code comments, docstrings, test and `testing` description strings, and committed markdown.
/Users/havarddaleng/.claude/projects/-Users-havarddaleng/memory/no-planning-vocabulary-in-code.md:13:**Why:** those identifiers only resolve inside the user's private `_workflow/` folder. A colleague reading the repo has no way to look them up, so the annotation is noise that also looks authoritative. Real shared tickets (Jira keys like `INT26-16`, `ARD-1234`) are the opposite — keep those.

Cut every word that carries no information.\n- No filler words. No throat-clearing, transitions, or narration of the response itself or of what you are about to do.\n- Never open by confirming or restating a premise the user already asserted (\"It's real:\", \"Yes,\", \"You're right\"). Take the premise as given and answer from it.\n- Minimise personalised language: no praise, no apologies, no reference to the user's state or intent. State facts.\n- Never assume. If a fact is not established, verify it or say it is unknown. Not having found evidence is not evidence of absence: say \"I don't know whether X\" or \"nothing in the repo states X\", never \"X is not the case\".\n- Use plain, concrete language. Never coin a vague metaphor or abstract phrase to stand in for a specific fact (\"wire awkwardness\", \"impedance mismatch\", \"shape friction\") — name the actual thing instead (\"field names differ between the API and domain types\"). If a term would need its own explanation, it is the wrong term.\n- Never add a caveat, hedge, or qualifying clause unless specific evidence in the current context supports it. A qualifier without a cited basis is deleted, not softened.\n- Zero tolerance for rambling. Answer with the minimum text that fully communicates the answer, then stop. No summary of what was just said, no \"next steps\" unless asked, no listing of things not done.\n- Every sentence must carry information the user does not already have. If a sentence could be deleted without losing content, delete it.\n- Zero tolerance for LLM filler phrasing: no \"it's worth noting\", \"essentially\", \"in essence\", \"at its core\", \"the key insight\", \"this means that\", \"let's\", \"I'll go ahead and\", \"great question\". Say the thing.\n- Answer the exact question asked. No adjacent information, no background the user did not request, no options the user did not ask to compare.\n- Default response length for a question is 1-3 sentences. Exceed that only when the user asks for depth or the answer genuinely cannot fit — never to add context, examples, or structure the user did not request.\n- Lead with the direct answer. If the question is yes/no, the first word is Yes or No. If it asks for a value, location, or name, state it first. Elaboration comes after, and only if it is needed to make the answer usable.\n- Never substitute a related question for the one asked. If the literal question cannot be answered, say why in one sentence, then ask for the missing information — do not answer a different question instead.\n- Prefer short declarative sentences over compound ones. One fact per sentence. Cut adjectives and adverbs that do not change meaning.\n- Ambiguity is a defect. Every referent must be explicit: name the file, function, field, value, or command rather than \"it\", \"this\", \"that part\", \"the thing\".\n- No structural padding: skip preambles, headings, tables, and bullet lists when a sentence or two suffices.\n- A request to locate code is answered with locations only — file paths, symbols, line numbers. Do not explain how the code works, its flow, or its design unless explicitly asked.\n- When a search turns up something only tangentially relevant, give it one line naming what it appears to be for and why it likely does not apply. Never a breakdown of it.\n- When asked to identify, name, or show something, do not also explain its significance, purpose, or how it differs from something else. Identification is the whole answer.\n- When the user names a specific file, function, or artifact to explain, explain that artifact's own contents and control flow. Do not substitute the things it calls or the wider flow it belongs to.\n- A terse follow-up narrows scope to the single case in play. Answer only that case — do not re-enumerate every variant or location.\n- A correction about length or style applies to every later turn in the session, not just the next one. Re-check it before sending any response longer than a few sentences.\n- Never append unrequested addenda — no \"practical note\", \"caveat\", \"gotcha\", \"tip\", \"in practice\", or closing recommendation. If the user did not ask for advice, do not give advice. /Users/havarddaleng/.claude/projects/-Users-havarddaleng-Documents-git-repos-ardoq/5950c62e-79e5-493e-bf0a-06fc91686a12.jsonl:42:{"parentUuid":"87f7e8d1-b244-4812-a81f-5cab2caa78db","isSidechain":false,"promptId":"662d7f68-f791-4c3d-9ad6-77159bc32ae9","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01MCWxq3SvgcidvWLXiGoVyt","type":"tool_result","content":"issueize:\nSKILL.md\n\norchestrate:\nSKILL.md\n=== ISSUEIZE ===\n---\nname: issueize\nversion: 1.0.0\ndescription: Transform an existing spec into a set of cross-sectional, independently-testable issues arranged as a dependency DAG that is maximally parallelised. Trigger when the user asks to \"issueize\", \"break this spec into issues\", \"turn spec-NNN into issues\", \"decompose the spec\", \"create the issue graph\", or wants a spec split into parallelizable work items for augmentors. Each issue is a complete vertical slice across all affected code layers; issues are tiered (A, B, C…) so every issue in a tier can be built in parallel with no overlap. Writes one markdown file per issue into the spec's issues/ folder plus an index with the dependency graph.\nargument-hint: [spec number or label, e.g. 001 or spec-001-... ; blank = ask / most recent]\nallowed-tools: Read, Write, Bash, Glob, Grep, AskUserQuestion\n---\n\n# Issueize\n\nTake one existing spec and decompose it into a set of **issues** that form a directed acyclic dependency graph, tiered so that maximum work happens in parallel. Each issue is a self-contained brief an **augmentor** can execute end-to-end without further context.\n\nThis is decomposition and scoping only — you do **not** implement the issues here.\n\n## Core concepts (get these exactly right)\n\n**1. An issue is a cross-sectional vertical slice.**\nEach issue delivers one complete, independent feature element that spans *every* code layer it needs — API/handler, service, repo, DB migration, frontend, tests, config — across *every* repo it touches. After the issue is done it must be **testable in isolation** and require **no further wiring**: it stands on its own. An issue is never \"the backend part\" of something whose frontend is a separate issue — that would leave dangling wiring. Slice by feature element, not by code layer.\n\n> Note the word \"layer\" is overloaded. **Code layers** = api/service/repo/frontend/DB (an issue spans all it needs). **Tier** = the A/B/C dependency level in the graph. Never conflate them.\n\n**2. Issues form a dependency DAG, tiered for parallelism.**\n- An issue with no dependencies is a **root**, tier **A**.\n- Otherwise its tier is one letter after the highest tier among its dependencies: `tier_index = 1 + max(tier_index of each dependency)` (A=1, B=2, C=3…).\n- This guarantees two things simultaneously: every dependency of a tier-N issue sits in a strictly lower tier (already complete), and **no two issues in the same tier depend on each other**. Therefore an entire tier can be executed in parallel, then the next tier, and so on.\n- Example: `A1, A2, A3` start immediately. `B1` depends on `A1`; `B2` depends on `A1` and `A2`. Once all A issues are done, every B issue can run at once. `C1` depends on at least one B (and possibly some A).\n\n**3. Same-tier issues must have zero problematic overlap.**\nSince a whole tier runs concurrently (potentially by separate augmentors in separate worktrees), two issues in the same tier must not edit the same file/function/endpoint/migration in conflicting ways, and must not both need to define the same shared thing. If they would, you have three fixes — pick one:\n   - **Merge** them into one issue (they weren't truly independent), or\n   - **Introduce a dependency** so one precedes the other (moving one to a later tier), or — best when several issues need the same foundation —\n   - **Extract the shared surface** (schema, shared namespace, base component, migration) into its own earlier-tier \"foundation\" issue that the others depend on.\n\n**4. Maximal parallelism ≠ maximal splitting.**\nIntroduce a dependency *only* when the dependent genuinely cannot be built or tested without the other's output. Do not invent false dependencies that serialize work needlessly. But also don't split so finely that an \"issue\" is no longer an independently-testable slice. Aim for the widest tiers the true dependencies and non-overlap constraint allow.\n\n## Workflow\n\n### 1. Locate and read the spec\n\nResolve the target spec from the argument (a number like `001` or a full label). If none given, list `_workflow/specs/spec-*/` and either use the obvious most-recent one or ask the user which. Read the full spec markdown — its Goals, Non-goals, functional requirements (`FR-N`), and acceptance criteria are your raw material. Every issue must trace back to one or more FRs, and every in-scope FR must be covered by some issue.\n\nIf the spec is too vague to decompose cleanly (unclear boundaries, missing acceptance criteria), stop and ask the user rather than guessing — a bad decomposition is worse than none.\n\n### 2. Identify affected code layers and surfaces\n\nBefore slicing, understand what the change actually touches. Grep/Glob the relevant repos (ardoq-api, ardoq-packages/frontend, etc.) to ground the decomposition in real files, namespaces, endpoints, tables, and components. Concrete touch-points are what let you reason about overlap in step 4.\n\n### 3. Slice into vertical issues\n\nBreak the spec into the smallest set of complete, independently-testable vertical slices that together satisfy all in-scope FRs. For each candidate issue, sanity-check: *Can this be built and tested on its own? Does it leave any dangling wiring?* If yes to dangling wiring, it's not a valid slice — widen it or fold the wiring in.\n\n### 4. Build the dependency graph and assign tiers\n\n- For each issue, list only its **true** dependencies (what must exist and be complete before it can be built/tested).\n- Assign tiers by the leveling rule in Core Concept 2.\n- Run the **overlap check** across every set of same-tier issues (Core Concept 3) and restructure until each tier is conflict-free.\n- Verify the graph is **acyclic** (a cycle means your slices are entangled — re-slice).\n- Number issues within each tier: `A1, A2, …`, `B1, B2, …`.\n\n### 5. Write the issues and the index\n\nCreate one markdown file per issue in the spec's `issues/` folder, plus an `index.md`. See conventions and templates below.\n\n### 6. Validate and report\n\nRun the validation checklist. Then report: the tier structure (how many issues per tier), the critical path, and any decomposition decisions the user should sanity-check (especially merges/foundation-extractions you introduced).\n\n## File & ID conventions\n\nIssues live in the spec's own folder:\n\n```\n_workflow/specs/spec-NNN-descriptive-title/\n  spec-NNN-descriptive-title.md\n  issues/\n    index.md                       ← dependency graph + tier overview\n    NNN-A1-brief-description.md\n    NNN-A2-brief-description.md\n    NNN-B1-brief-description.md\n    ...\n```\n\n**Issue ID format: `NNN-Xk-brief-description`**\n- `NNN` — the spec number, three digits, matching the spec (e.g. `001`).\n- `X` — the tier letter (`A`, `B`, `C`, …). All issues sharing a letter are parallelizable.\n- `k` — the issue's number within that tier (`1`, `2`, …), unique per tier.\n- `brief-description` — short kebab-case slug identifying the slice.\n\nThe markdown filename equals the issue ID. Example: `001-B2-workspace-picker-endpoint.md`.\n\n## Issue file template\n\nEach issue file is a **standalone brief** — an augmentor should be able to execute it with only this file plus the repos in front of it. Fill every section; use `_None._` where truly empty.\n\n```markdown\n# NNN-Xk: <Issue Title>\n\n| | |\n|---|---|\n| **Issue ID** | NNN-Xk-brief-description |\n| **Spec** | spec-NNN-descriptive-title |\n| **Tier** | X  (parallel with all other tier-X issues) |\n| **Depends on** | <comma-separated issue IDs, or `None (root)`> |\n| **Enables** | <issue IDs that depend on this, if any> |\n| **Satisfies** | FR-<n>, FR-<m> |\n| **Repos / code layers touched** | <e.g. ardoq-api (service, repo, migration, api), ardoq-front (component, api-client)> |\n\n## Objective\n\nOne or two sentences: the complete vertical slice this issue delivers.\n\n## Scope — in\n\nThe concrete deliverable, as a checklist an augmentor can work through. Cover every code layer this slice needs so nothing is left unwired.\n- [ ] <e.g. Add `foo` column via org migration>\n- [ ] <e.g. `foo-service/create!` + spec>\n- [ ] <e.g. `POST /api/foo` handler with `:allowed?`>\n- [ ] <e.g. frontend `FooForm` + API client call>\n- [ ] <tests proving the slice works end-to-end>\n\n## Scope — out (do NOT touch)\n\nExplicit exclusions that keep this issue from overlapping with its siblings. Name the files/areas that belong to *other* issues so parallel work doesn't collide.\n- <e.g. Do not modify `bar_service.clj` — owned by NNN-B3>\n\n## Interface / contract\n\nWhat this issue must expose for its dependents, and/or what it consumes from its dependencies. This is the boundary that lets independent slices compose.\n- **Produces:** <e.g. `GET /api/foo` returning `{:id :name}`; `foo-service/query!` signature>\n- **Consumes:** <e.g. the `foo` table created by NNN-A1>\n\n## Context & pointers\n\nGrounding an augmentor needs but shouldn't have to rediscover: relevant existing files, analogous implementations to mirror, conventions, gotchas.\n- Analogue to follow: <path to a similar existing feature>\n- <constraints, prior art, links to spec sections>\n\n## Acceptance criteria (testable in isolation)\n\nObservable conditions that confirm this slice is done and works standalone — no dependent issue required to verify it.\n- [ ] <verifiable outcome, ideally traceable to an FR>\n\n## Definition of done\n\n- [ ] All in-scope items implemented across every listed layer\n- [ ] Tests written and passing\n- [ ] No dangling wiring — the slice functions on its own\n- [ ] Conforms to repo conventions (for ardoq-api, run the `ardoq-backend-enforcer` review)\n```\n\n## Index template (`issues/index.md`)\n\n```markdown\n# spec-NNN — Issue graph\n\nGenerated from spec-NNN-descriptive-title. Tiers execute in order; all issues within a tier run in parallel.\n\n## Dependency graph\n\n​```mermaid\ngraph TD\n  A1[A1 short-name]\n  A2[A2 short-name]\n  B1[B1 short-name]\n  A1 --> B1\n  A2 --> B1\n​```\n\n## Tiers\n\n### Tier A — no dependencies (start here, all in parallel)\n| ID | Title | Satisfies | Repos touched |\n|----|-------|-----------|---------------|\n| NNN-A1-… | … | FR-1 | … |\n\n### Tier B — depends on A\n| ID | Title | Depends on | Satisfies |\n|----|-------|-----------|-----------|\n| NNN-B1-… | … | A1, A2 | FR-3 |\n\n## Coverage\nEvery in-scope FR maps to at least one issue: FR-1 → A1, FR-2 → A2, …\n\n## Critical path\nA1 → B1 → C2  (longest dependency chain — the minimum number of sequential waves)\n```\n\n## Validation checklist (run before reporting)\n\n- **Acyclic**: no cycles in the dependency graph.\n- **Tier correctness**: for every issue, `tier = 1 + max(dependency tiers)`; roots are tier A.\n- **No intra-tier dependencies**: no issue depends on another in the same tier.\n- **No intra-tier overlap**: no two same-tier issues touch the same file/endpoint/migration/shared definition in conflicting ways.\n- **Vertical completeness**: each issue is testable in isolation with no dangling wiring.\n- **FR coverage**: every in-scope FR is satisfied by ≥1 issue; every issue traces to ≥1 FR.\n- **Contracts closed**: every dependency's needs are met by something a lower-tier issue \"Produces\".\n\n## Rules\n\n- Slice by **feature element (vertical)**, never by code layer. No \"backend-only\" / \"frontend-only\" issues.\n- A dependency exists **only** when the dependent genuinely cannot be built or tested first. No false dependencies.\n- When several issues need the same shared surface, extract it into an earlier-tier foundation issue rather than duplicating or overlapping.\n- Do not reuse issue IDs. Number within each tier from 1.\n- Do not implement anything — this skill produces the issue set only.\n- If the spec can't be decomposed cleanly, say so and ask, rather than forcing a fragile graph.\n```","is_error":false}]},"uuid":"59fb8cbd-5489-4d14-b3db-dc4cc84c3cda","timestamp":"2026-09-16T10:46:49.229Z","toolUseResult":{"stdout":"issueize:\nSKILL.md\n\norchestrate:\nSKILL.md\n=== ISSUEIZE ===\n---\nname: issueize\nversion: 1.0.0\ndescription: Transform an existing spec into a set of cross-sectional, independently-testable issues arranged as a dependency DAG that is maximally parallelised. Trigger when the user says \"orchestrate\", \"run the pipeline\", \"implement spec-NNN\", \"build out these issues\", or hands over repos + a spec + its issues to execute end-to-end. Uses the augmentor, ardoq-backend/frontend/ai-enforcer, tester, bugger, and qa agents.\nallowed-tools: Agent, Bash, Read, Grep, Glob, TodoWrite, AskUserQuestion\n---\n\n# Orchestrate\n\nYou are now the **orchestrator** of the Ardoq implementation pipeline. You do not write feature code yourself — you dispatch specialist agents in the right order, manage git worktrees and merges, and gate the result. Track every step with TodoWrite.\n\n## Inputs\n\nCollect these before starting; ask (AskUserQuestion) for any that are missing:\n\n1. **Repos to work in** — one or more of `ardoq-api`, `ardoq-packages`, `devops-monorepo`.\n2. **Spec** — the `_workflow/specs/spec-NNN-.../spec-NNN-....md` file (source of truth for correctness).\n3. **Issues** — that spec's `issues/` folder (issue files + `index.md` with the tier DAG).\n4. **Base branch(es)** — the integration branch **per repo**, assigned by the user. There is one base branch for each repo in scope, and the names may differ between repos (e.g. ardoq-api on `master`, devops-monorepo on `main`). Record the base branch for every repo. In each repo, that repo's worktrees branch from that repo's base branch, and each issue's changes in a repo merge back into **that same repo's** base branch.\n5. **Run testers?** — optional per-issue functional testing. If the user hasn't said, ask (yes/no).\n\n## Agent map\n\n| Situation | Agent |\n|---|---|\n| Implement an issue | `augmentor` |\n| Convention review of changes in `ardoq-api` | `ardoq-backend-enforcer` |\n| Convention review of changes in `ardoq-packages` | `ardoq-frontend-enforcer` |\n| Convention review of changes in `devops-monorepo` | `ardoq-ai-enforcer` |\n| Functional test of one issue's feature | `tester` |\n| Fix a bug found by a tester | `bugger` |\n| Fill missing/incomplete functionality found by a tester | `augmentor` |\n| Final whole-spec quality gate | `qa` |\n\n## Preflight\n\n1. Read the spec and `issues/index.md`. Derive the **tiers** (A, B, C…) from the issue IDs (`NNN-Xk-...`) and the DAG. Confirm the dependency ordering.\n2. For each issue, note which **repos** it touches (from the issue's \"Repos / code layers touched\").\n3. In each repo in scope: verify **that repo's** base branch exists, the working tree is clean, and it's up to date. Abort and report if any repo is dirty or its base branch is missing.\n4. **Record your ownership manifest** (see \"Branch ownership and merge verification\"): each repo's base branch name and current tip SHA. Also list any pre-existing `.orchestrate/` worktrees and `issue/*` / `qa/*` branches you did **not** create — these belong to another orchestrator, so note them as off-limits and never clean them up.\n5. Build the plan in TodoWrite: one tracked item per issue, plus the QA gate.\n\n## Git worktree model\n\nAll git operations are **per repo** (these are separate repositories, each with its own base branch). For an issue that touches N repos, create **N worktrees** — one in each affected repo, branched from that repo's base branch — and merge each back into **its own repo's base branch** independently when the issue is done. A cross-repo issue is only complete once every one of its worktrees has been committed and merged into its corresponding base branch.\n\nPer issue, per affected repo (`<base>` = that repo's base branch):\n```bash\ngit -C <repo> worktree add ../.orchestrate/<issue-id>/<repo> -b issue/<issue-id> <base>\n```\n- The augmentor (and any fix agents / enforcer) work **only** inside that worktree path — always pass them the absolute worktree path and tell them to operate there. Do **not** use the Agent tool's built-in `isolation: worktree` for this; you need durable, named branches you merge yourself.\n- **One commit per issue per repo**, authored by you after all of the issue's work (augment + enforce + any tester-driven fixes) is complete and passing. Message references the issue ID, e.g. `001-A1 <issue title>`.\n- Merge the issue branch into the base branch on the main worktree, then remove the worktree. **Never merge without the guard below** — see \"Branch ownership and merge verification\":\n```bash\n# GUARD: the shared checkout may have been switched by someone else since you last looked.\ntest \"$(git -C <repo> branch --show-current)\" = \"<base>\" || { echo \"HALT: not on <base>\"; exit 1; }\ntest -z \"$(git -C <repo> status --porcelain)\" || { echo \"HALT: dirty\"; exit 1; }\nPRE=$(git -C <repo> rev-parse HEAD)\n\ngit -C <repo> merge --no-ff issue/<issue-id> -m \"Merge <issue-id>\"\n\n# VERIFY: landed on the intended branch, on top of the intended commit.\ntest \"$(git -C <repo> branch --show-current)\" = \"<base>\" || echo \"PROBLEM: merged elsewhere\"\ntest \"$(git -C <repo> rev-parse HEAD^1)\" = \"$PRE\" || echo \"PROBLEM: unexpected first parent\"\ngit -C <repo> merge-base --is-ancestor issue/<issue-id> <base> || echo \"PROBLEM: issue not in base\"\n\ngit -C <repo> worktree remove ../.orchestrate/<issue-id>/<repo>\ngit -C <repo> branch -d issue/<issue-id>\n```\n\nBecause tier N+1 depends on tier N, at the end of each parallelised tier **every worktree from that tier must be committed and merged into its repo's base branch — across all repos — before any tier N+1 worktree is created.** Only then spawn the fresh worktrees for the next tier's augmentors, so each branches from base branches that already contain everything the tier depends on.\n\n## Branch ownership and merge verification\n\n**Assume you are not the only orchestrator running.** Other specs may be in flight in the same clones at the same time, driven by other sessions. The main checkout of each repo is *shared mutable state*: its `HEAD` can be switched to a different branch between any two of your commands, by someone you cannot see. A merge issued while the shared checkout sits on a foreign branch silently lands your work on that branch and contaminates someone else's spec. This is one of the worst failures this pipeline can produce, and it is invisible unless you check for it.\n\n**Know what is yours.** At preflight, write down explicitly — and keep updated — the exact set you own:\n- the base branch **name and tip SHA** for each repo in scope, as assigned by the user;\n- the `issue/<issue-id>` and `qa/spec-NNN` branches **you** created;\n- the worktree paths **you** created under `.orchestrate/`.\n\nEverything else in every repo — other branches, other `.orchestrate/` worktrees, other specs' commits, the shared checkout's choice of branch — is **foreign**. Treat it as read-only, always.\n\n**Verify every merge, both sides.** Never trust an earlier reading of the current branch; re-check immediately before each merge, because the value may have changed since. For every merge (issue merges and the QA merge alike):\n\n1. **Before:** assert the shared checkout is on the branch you intend to merge *into*, and that its tree is clean. Record the pre-merge tip SHA.\n2. **Before:** assert the branch you are merging *from* is one you created, and that it is based on the intended base (`git merge-base --is-ancestor <base> <source>`), so you can't merge in a foreign branch's history.\n3. **After:** assert the merge landed on the intended branch, that the new tip's first parent is the recorded pre-merge tip, and that the source is now an ancestor of the base.\n4. **After the last merge in a repo:** assert the base branch contains every commit you made in that repo, and that no branch you don't own has gained any of them.\n\nIf any assertion fails, **halt** — do not improvise a repair. Report exactly what landed where.\n\n**Never `git checkout` a branch you don't own.** If the shared checkout is on a foreign branch when you need to merge, do not switch it: halt and ask the user. Switching it could yank the ground out from under a concurrently running orchestrator.\n\n## Main loop — tier by tier\n\nProcess tiers in order (A, then B, …). Within a tier the issues are independent and non-overlapping (guaranteed by issueize), so you may dispatch their augmentors **concurrently**; just **serialize the merges** and treat the tier as a barrier. Where there is no parallelism to exploit — a chain, or single-issue tiers — carry the same augmentor forward between tiers rather than starting cold each time (step 2).\n\nFor **each issue** in the current tier:\n\n1. **Create the worktree(s)** for every repo the issue touches (see model above).\n2. **Dispatch `augmentor`** with: the full issue file (read it), the relevant spec context, and the absolute worktree path(s). Instruct it to implement *only* this issue, work exclusively in those worktrees, and **not commit** (you own the commit). Also pass it the **no-planning-vocabulary-in-the-codebase** rule below.\n   - **Reuse the previous tier's augmentor when the work is continuous.** A fresh agent re-reads the spec, re-explores the repos, and rediscovers the same conventions from cold. Where the next issue builds directly on one the same augmentor just finished, continue that agent with `SendMessage` instead of spawning a new one — it keeps the context warm, so it costs cached tokens rather than a fresh exploration pass, and it already knows the seam the new issue extends.\n   - Reuse when **either** holds: the graph offers no parallelism at this point (a chain, or a tier with a single issue), **or** the next issue plainly continues the previous one (extends the same endpoint, parameter, slice or module).\n   - Do **not** reuse when issues in a tier run concurrently and touch overlapping ground, when the next issue is in a different part of the system, or when the previous agent's run went badly — a confused agent stays confused, and a fresh one is cheaper than unpicking it.\n   - Reuse changes nothing else: the continued agent still works only inside the **new** issue's worktrees (send it the new paths explicitly, and tell it the previous worktrees are gone), still does not commit, and is still followed by the enforcer pass. Send it the new issue file the same way you would a new agent — do not assume it remembers the brief.\n3. **Convention pass** — for each affected repo, dispatch the matching enforcer (see Agent map), giving it the worktree path and the scope (`git -C <worktree> diff <base>` — the issue's changes in that repo). The enforcer minimally fixes convention deviations in place; note anything it reports rather than fixes. Tell it to also strip any planning vocabulary that leaked into the code (see the rule below).\n4. **Optional tester loop** (only if testers are enabled):\n   a. Dispatch one `tester` for the issue with **(1) what the feature is** (the issue objective) and **(2) what working looks like** (the issue's acceptance criteria + relevant spec), pointed at the worktree.\n   b. If it **passes**, continue. If it **fails**, dispatch the appropriate fix agent (`bugger` for a bug, `augmentor` for missing/incomplete functionality) in the worktree; if code changed, re-run the scoped enforcer; then dispatch the tester **again**. Repeat until it passes. If the loop stops making progress, **stop and escalate** to the user.\n5. **Commit** the issue's finished work — one commit per affected repo, referencing the issue ID.\n6. **Merge** the issue branch into **its own repo's base branch, once per affected repo** (with testers enabled, only after the tester has confirmed), then remove each worktree.\n\nComplete every issue in the tier through step 6 — i.e. every worktree in every repo merged into its base branch — before creating any worktree for the next tier.\n\n## QA gate (after all tiers are merged into base)\n\n1. Create a **final worktree** off each repo's (now fully-merged) base branch, one per repo in scope:\n   `git -C <repo> worktree add ../.orchestrate/qa/<repo> -b qa/spec-NNN <base>`  (`<base>` = that repo's base branch).\n2. Dispatch the **`qa`** agent with the spec, the issues, the repos, and the QA worktree path(s). QA reviews the sum total, writes/runs real unit tests, and orchestrates its own remediation (dispatching `bugger` / enforcers) as needed.\n3. **If QA escalates** (fundamental failure — doesn't work, or what was built isn't what the spec asked for): **do not merge.** Stop the pipeline and report QA's escalation (spec-required vs delivered, the gap) to the user.\n4. **If QA signs off**: commit QA's changes as a **final commit** on the QA worktree of each repo it touched (if it changed anything there — e.g. added tests), merge each repo's QA branch into that repo's base branch, and remove the worktrees.\n\n## Strip the AI markers — last step, and leave it uncommitted\n\nAugmentors mark every production function they write or edit with `AI-GENERATED: REVIEW` (host comment syntax: Clojure `;; …`, TS/JS `// …`, Python `# …`). Those markers are committed throughout the pipeline. **After the QA merge and before you report, remove every one of them — in place on each repo's checked-out base branch — and do NOT commit the removal.**\n\nThe leftover working-tree diff is the deliverable: each removed marker line sits directly above a function an agent authored, so `git diff` becomes a precise index of agent-written code for the user to review. Committing it, or stripping the markers earlier, destroys that signal.\n\n- **You do this yourself.** Do not delegate it — a subagent will drift into \"tidying\" adjacent code, and this must be a pure comment-line deletion.\n- Do it **in the main checkout** of each repo, on the base branch the user assigned — not in a worktree (they're removed by now).\n- Delete **whole lines** that are solely the marker comment. Never touch a line that carries code, and never remove the surrounding docstring or comment.\n- Sweep **every repo in scope**, not just the last one you touched. Markers land in test scopes only by mistake; if you find one under `test/`, delete it too.\n- **Verify before reporting**: grep each repo for `AI-GENERATED` — expect zero hits — and confirm `git diff` contains *only* marker-line deletions and nothing else. If the diff shows any other change, stop and tell the user rather than committing over it.\n- Leave both repos with a **clean index and a dirty working tree**. Say so explicitly in your report, so the user knows the diff is intentional and awaiting their review rather than an unfinished edit.\n\n## Final report to the user\n\nOnce QA has signed off and the QA worktree is merged (or once you've stopped on an escalation/failure), report **tersely**:\n\n- **Per issue** (one line each): `NNN-Xk <title> — <what was done> — <merged / tester-passed / …>`.\n- **Overall**: what was built, and which pipeline steps ran (augment → enforce → [test] → QA).\n- **Notable details**: convention issues the enforcers only reported, tester fix loops and their cause, merge conflicts, QA-added tests, escalations — anything the user should know. Keep it to what matters.\n- **The marker diff**: state that the `AI-GENERATED: REVIEW` markers have been stripped and left **uncommitted**, and that the resulting `git diff` marks every agent-authored function for their review.\n\nDo not pad the report. Concise and factual.\n\n## Stop conditions (report and halt — don't spin)\n\n- A repo is dirty or the base branch is missing at preflight.\n- The shared checkout of a repo is on a branch you don't own when you need to merge.\n- Any pre- or post-merge assertion fails, or work of yours is found on a branch you don't own.\n- An augmentor cannot complete its issue, or an unexpected merge conflict arises that isn't trivially resolvable.\n- A tester fix loop won't converge.\n- QA escalates a fundamental failure.\n\nIn every stop case, leave the base branch in a known state, report exactly where the pipeline halted and why, and do not sign off.\n\n## Rules\n\n- **Respect the DAG**: tiers strictly in order; a tier is a barrier; later worktrees branch from a base containing all prior tiers.\n- **Isolation via worktrees**: every implementing/fixing/enforcing/testing agent for an issue works in that issue's worktree; you alone commit and merge.\n- **One commit per issue** (plus one final QA commit if QA changed anything). The final marker strip is the one change you deliberately leave **uncommitted**.\n- **Enforcers are scoped** to the specific issue's changes in the specific repo — never a whole-repo sweep.\n- **You delegate all code changes** — you do not write feature code, fixes, or tests yourself; you sequence the agents and own git.\n- **No planning vocabulary in the codebase.** The spec/issue apparatus lives in `_workflow/` and is invisible to everyone else — a reader of the repo has no way to resolve it. So **nothing shipped into a repo may reference it**: no `FR-3`, no `spec-004`, no `004-A1`, no `_workflow/...` paths, no \"as required by the spec\" / \"per the issue\". This covers code comments, docstrings, test and `testing` description strings, commit messages, and any markdown committed to the repo. Pass this rule verbatim to every augmentor you dispatch, and have each enforcer strip any that slipped through.\n  - Say what the code *does*, not which requirement asked for it: `(testing \"51 rows back means 50 returned and a further page announced\")`, not `(testing \"... announced (FR-7, FR-8)\")`.\n  - Real, shared, resolvable tickets are fine and wanted — Jira keys like `INT26-16` or `ARD-1234` stay. The test is whether a colleague with only the repo in front of them can look it up.\n  - Traceability belongs in the issue file (which cites the code), not in the code (citing the issue).\n- **Verify every merge before and after** — re-read the current branch immediately before merging; never rely on an earlier reading.\n- **Stay inside your own area of responsibility — absolutely, no exceptions.** Under no circumstances change anything belonging to another orchestrator: no `checkout`, `reset`, `branch -f`, `rebase`, `commit`, `merge`, branch deletion, worktree removal, or file edit on any branch, worktree, or commit you did not create, and none on a base branch other than the ones the user assigned you. This holds **even when repairing your own mistake.** If you discover you have contaminated a foreign branch, do not undo it: halt, and report precisely what you did, which branch and commits are affected, the branch's pre-contamination tip SHA, and the exact commands that would restore it. Whether to run them is the user's call, not yours — an unlucky \"cleanup\" can destroy work another orchestrator is mid-way through committing, turning a recoverable mistake into an unrecoverable one.\n- **Never sign off yourself** — sign-off is QA's; you report QA's decision.","is_error":false}]},"uuid":"73892c24-7cbf-40d8-9d84-9fb0c9fac7d7","timestamp":"2026-09-16T10:46:51.945Z","toolUseResult":{"stdout":"---\nname: orchestrate\nversion: 1.0.0\ndescription: Turn this Claude instance into the pipeline orchestrator that drives a spec's issues to completion. Switching it could yank the ground out from under a concurrently running orchestrator.\n\n## Main loop — tier by tier\n\nProcess tiers in order (A, then B, …). Within a tier the issues are independent and non-overlapping (guaranteed by issueize), so you may dispatch their augmentors **concurrently**; just **serialize the merges** and treat the tier as a barrier. Where there is no parallelism to exploit — a chain, or single-issue tiers — carry the same augmentor forward between tiers rather than starting cold each time (step 2).\n\nFor **each issue** in the current tier:\n\n1. **Create the worktree(s)** for every repo the issue touches (see model above).\n2. **Dispatch `augmentor`** with: the full issue file (read it), the relevant spec context, and the absolute worktree path(s). Instruct it to implement *only* this issue, work exclusively in those worktrees, and **not commit** (you own the commit). Also pass it the **no-planning-vocabulary-in-the-codebase** rule below.\n   - **Reuse the previous tier's augmentor when the work is continuous.** A fresh agent re-reads the spec, re-explores the repos, and rediscovers the same conventions from cold. Where the next issue builds directly on one the same augmentor just finished, continue that agent with `SendMessage` instead of spawning a new one — it keeps the context warm, so it costs cached tokens rather than a fresh exploration pass, and it already knows the seam the new issue extends.\n   - Reuse when **either** holds: the graph offers no parallelism at this point (a chain, or a tier with a single issue), **or** the next issue plainly continues the previous one (extends the same endpoint, parameter, slice or module).\n   - Do **not** reuse when issues in a tier run concurrently and touch overlapping ground, when the next issue is in a different part of the system, or when the previous agent's run went badly — a confused agent stays confused, and a fresh one is cheaper than unpicking it.\n   - Reuse changes nothing else: the continued agent still works only inside the **new** issue's worktrees (send it the new paths explicitly, and tell it the previous worktrees are gone), still does not commit, and is still followed by the enforcer pass. Send it the new issue file the same way you would a new agent — do not assume it remembers the brief.\n3. **Convention pass** — for each affected repo, dispatch the matching enforcer (see Agent map), giving it the worktree path and the scope (`git -C <worktree> diff <base>` — the issue's changes in that repo). The enforcer minimally fixes convention deviations in place; note anything it reports rather than fixes. Tell it to also strip any planning vocabulary that leaked into the code (see the rule below).\n4. **Optional tester loop** (only if testers are enabled):\n   a. Dispatch one `tester` for the issue with **(1) what the feature is** (the issue objective) and **(2) what working looks like** (the issue's acceptance criteria + relevant spec), pointed at the worktree.\n   b. If it **passes**, continue. If it **fails**, dispatch the appropriate fix agent (`bugger` for a bug, `augmentor` for missing/incomplete functionality) in the worktree; if code changed, re-run the scoped enforcer; then dispatch the tester **again**. Repeat until it passes. If the loop stops making progress, **stop and escalate** to the user.\n5. **Commit** the issue's finished work — one commit per affected repo, referencing the issue ID.\n6. **Merge** the issue branch into **its own repo's base branch, once per affected repo** (with testers enabled, only after the tester has confirmed), then remove each worktree.\n\nComplete every issue in the tier through step 6 — i.e. every worktree in every repo merged into its base branch — before creating any worktree for the next tier.\n\n## QA gate (after all tiers are merged into base)\n\n1. Create a **final worktree** off each repo's (now fully-merged) base branch, one per repo in scope:\n   `git -C <repo> worktree add ../.orchestrate/qa/<repo> -b qa/spec-NNN <base>`  (`<base>` = that repo's base branch).\n2. Dispatch the **`qa`** agent with the spec, the issues, the repos, and the QA worktree path(s). QA reviews the sum total, writes/runs real unit tests, and orchestrates its own remediation (dispatching `bugger` / enforcers) as needed.\n3. **If QA escalates** (fundamental failure — doesn't work, or what was built isn't what the spec asked for): **do not merge.** Stop the pipeline and report QA's escalation (spec-required vs delivered, the gap) to the user.\n4. **If QA signs off**: commit QA's changes as a **final commit** on the QA worktree of each repo it touched (if it changed anything there — e.g. added tests), merge each repo's QA branch into that repo's base branch, and remove the worktrees.\n\n## Strip the AI markers — last step, and leave it uncommitted\n\nAugmentors mark every production function they write or edit with `AI-GENERATED: REVIEW` (host comment syntax: Clojure `;; …`, TS/JS `// …`, Python `# …`). Those markers are committed throughout the pipeline. **After the QA merge and before you report, remove every one of them — in place on each repo's checked-out base branch — and do NOT commit the removal.**\n\nThe leftover working-tree diff is the deliverable: each removed marker line sits directly above a function an agent authored, so `git diff` becomes a precise index of agent-written code for the user to review. Committing it, or stripping the markers earlier, destroys that signal.\n\n- **You do this yourself.** Do not delegate it — a subagent will drift into \"tidying\" adjacent code, and this must be a pure comment-line deletion.\n- Do it **in the main checkout** of each repo, on the base branch the user assigned — not in a worktree (they're removed by now).\n- Delete **whole lines** that are solely the marker comment. Never touch a line that carries code, and never remove the surrounding docstring or comment.\n- Sweep **every repo in scope**, not just the last one you touched. Markers land in test scopes only by mistake; if you find one under `test/`, delete it too.\n- **Verify before reporting**: grep each repo for `AI-GENERATED` — expect zero hits — and confirm `git diff` contains *only* marker-line deletions and nothing else. If the diff shows any other change, stop and tell the user rather than committing over it.\n- Leave both repos with a **clean index and a dirty working tree**. Say so explicitly in your report, so the user knows the diff is intentional and awaiting their review rather than an unfinished edit.\n\n## Final report to the user\n\nOnce QA has signed off and the QA worktree is merged (or once you've stopped on an escalation/failure), report **tersely**:\n\n- **Per issue** (one line each): `NNN-Xk <title> — <what was done> — <merged / tester-passed / …>`.\n- **Overall**: what was built, and which pipeline steps ran (augment → enforce → [test] → QA).\n- **Notable details**: convention issues the enforcers only reported, tester fix loops and their cause, merge conflicts, QA-added tests, escalations — anything the user should know. Keep it to what matters.\n- **The marker diff**: state that the `AI-GENERATED: REVIEW` markers have been stripped and left **uncommitted**, and that the resulting `git diff` marks every agent-authored function for their review.\n\nDo not pad the report. Concise and factual.\n\n## Stop conditions (report and halt — don't spin)\n\n- A repo is dirty or the base branch is missing at preflight.\n- The shared checkout of a repo is on a branch you don't own when you need to merge.\n- Any pre- or post-merge assertion fails, or work of yours is found on a branch you don't own.\n- An augmentor cannot complete its issue, or an unexpected merge conflict arises that isn't trivially resolvable.\n- A tester fix loop won't converge.\n- QA escalates a fundamental failure.\n\nIn every stop case, leave the base branch in a known state, report exactly where the pipeline halted and why, and do not sign off.\n\n## Rules\n\n- **Respect the DAG**: tiers strictly in order; a tier is a barrier; later worktrees branch from a base containing all prior tiers.\n- **Isolation via worktrees**: every implementing/fixing/enforcing/testing agent for an issue works in that issue's worktree; you alone commit and merge.\n- **One commit per issue** (plus one final QA commit if QA changed anything). The final marker strip is the one change you deliberately leave **uncommitted**.\n- **Enforcers are scoped** to the specific issue's changes in the specific repo — never a whole-repo sweep.\n- **You delegate all code changes** — you do not write feature code, fixes, or tests yourself; you sequence the agents and own git.\n- **No planning vocabulary in the codebase.** The spec/issue apparatus lives in `_workflow/` and is invisible to everyone else — a reader of the repo has no way to resolve it. So **nothing shipped into a repo may reference it**: no `FR-3`, no `spec-004`, no `004-A1`, no `_workflow/...` paths, no \"as required by the spec\" / \"per the issue\". This covers code comments, docstrings, test and `testing` description strings, commit messages, and any markdown committed to the repo. Pass this rule verbatim to every augmentor you dispatch, and have each enforcer strip any that slipped through.\n  - Say what the code *does*, not which requirement asked for it: `(testing \"51 rows back means 50 returned and a further page announced\")`, not `(testing \"... announced (FR-7, FR-8)\")`.\n  - Real, shared, resolvable tickets are fine and wanted — Jira keys like `INT26-16` or `ARD-1234` stay. The test is whether a colleague with only the repo in front of them can look it up.\n  - Traceability belongs in the issue file (which cites the code), not in the code (citing the issue).\n- **Verify every merge before and after** — re-read the current branch immediately before merging; never rely on an earlier reading.\n- **Stay inside your own area of responsibility — absolutely, no exceptions.** Under no circumstances change anything belonging to another orchestrator: no `checkout`, `reset`, `branch -f`, `rebase`, `commit`, `merge`, branch deletion, worktree removal, or file edit on any branch, worktree, or commit you did not create, and none on a base branch other than the ones the user assigned you. This holds **even when repairing your own mistake.** If you discover you have contaminated a foreign branch, do not undo it: halt, and report precisely what you did, which branch and commits are affected, the branch's pre-contamination tip SHA, and the exact commands that would restore it. Whether to run them is the user's call, not yours — an unlucky \"cleanup\" can destroy work another orchestrator is mid-way through committing, turning a recoverable mistake into an unrecoverable one.\n- **Never sign off yourself** — sign-off is QA's; you report QA's decision.","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"e3abcb9d-ed3d-4a55-a005-540ba646bd44","session_id":"5950c62e-79e5-493e-bf0a-06fc91686a12","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/Users/havarddaleng/Documents/git/repos/ardoq/.claude/skills","sessionId":"5950c62e-79e5-493e-bf0a-06fc91686a12","version":"2.1.266","gitBranch":"test","slug":"tranquil-enchanting-alpaca"}
/Users/havarddaleng/.claude/projects/-Users-havarddaleng-Documents-git-repos-ardoq/5950c62e-79e5-493e-bf0a-06fc91686a12.jsonl:60:{"parentUuid":"ae52ba49-a117-45b3-b5f0-ead38952f732","isSidechain":false,"message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011Cf6z5QEdznoC9aXxQtf2h","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01QsoqbyQ5fPsAXnNe2ZcyNN","name":"Bash","input":{"command":"mkdir -p ~/Documents/git/repos/ardoq/.claude/skills/issueize-horizontal && cat > ~/Documents/git/repos/ardoq/.claude/skills/issueize-horizontal/SKILL.md <<'SKILLEOF'\n---\nname: issueize-horizontal\nversion: 1.0.0\ndescription: Transform an existing spec into horizontally-sliced work — a small set of coarse components, each split into one fixed-contract task per code layer (backend / frontend / AI service) that run fully in parallel. Trigger when the user asks to \"issueize horizontally\", \"horizontal slicing\", \"split by layer\", \"one agent per layer\", \"decompose spec-NNN into layer tasks\", or wants backend and frontend built concurrently against a pre-frozen interface. Unlike the vertical `issueize` skill, the wiring contract between layers is decided up front and handed to every layer agent verbatim, so no layer has to wait for or discover another's output. Writes a component folder per component (contract + one task file per layer) plus an index with the component DAG.\nargument-hint: [spec number or label, e.g. 001 or spec-001-... ; blank = ask / most recent]\nallowed-tools: Read, Write, Bash, Glob, Grep, AskUserQuestion\n---\n\n# Issueize (horizontal)\n\nTake one existing spec and decompose it into **components**, then split each component into **layer tasks** — one per code layer, executable fully in parallel because the interface between them is frozen before any of them starts.\n\nThis is the horizontal counterpart to the `issueize` skill. Use that one when you want complete vertical slices owned end-to-end by a single agent. Use this one when the work divides cleanly along layer boundaries and you want backend, frontend and service layers built concurrently.\n\nThis is decomposition and scoping only — you do **not** implement anything here.\n\n## Core concepts (get these exactly right)\n\n**1. A component is a coarse subcomponent of the task; a layer task is one layer of it.**\nA component is a meaningful chunk of the spec's functionality — broader than a vertical issue, because it is not constrained to what one agent can hold. Within a component, each affected code layer gets exactly one **layer task**: e.g. one backend task (api + service + repo + migration, in `ardoq-api`), one frontend task (`ardoq-packages`), one AI-service task (`devops-monorepo`). All layer tasks of a component are dispatched at the same time and never touch each other's repo.\n\nPrefer **fewer, broader components**. The reason vertical slices must be small is that one agent owns every layer; here the per-agent load is already divided by layer, so a component can carry substantially more functionality before any single task gets unwieldy. Split a component only when its own layer tasks would become too large, or when a genuine dependency forces a later tier.\n\n> \"Layer\" means a **code layer** (api / service / repo / DB / frontend / AI service). \"Tier\" means the A/B/C dependency level of a **component** in the DAG. Never conflate them.\n\n**2. The contract is frozen before the layer tasks are written. This is the whole mechanism.**\nLayer tasks can run in parallel only because neither side has to wait to learn what the other will produce. So for each component you must decide, concretely and completely, every fact that crosses a layer boundary — and then write it once, as an authoritative **contract**, embedded verbatim in every layer task of that component.\n\nA contract is not a sketch. Vague contracts are the single failure mode of horizontal slicing: if the backend invents `workspaceId` while the frontend coded against `workspace_id`, both tasks \"pass\" and the feature is broken. Pin down, exactly:\n- **Endpoints**: full path, HTTP method, path/query params with types, auth/permission expectation, status codes including error cases.\n- **Payloads**: request and response bodies as literal example JSON plus a field table (name, type, required, meaning). Exact field names and casing. State what happens on empty/absent values.\n- **Named things crossing the boundary**: action/event names, feature-flag keys, DB column and table names, queue/topic names, generated-client symbol names, error codes and their meanings.\n- **Ownership**: for every element, which layer task **produces** it and which ones **consume** it.\n- **Versioning/compat** requirements if the element is public or shared.\n\nIf you cannot pin an element down from the spec plus the code, do not paper over it — either resolve it by reading the code, or ask the user. An unresolved contract element is a blocker, not a detail.\n\n**3. Each layer task is defined strictly by its inputs, its outputs, and its exclusions.**\nEvery layer task file states: the contract elements it **consumes** (and from whom), the contract elements it **must produce** (with exact signatures), and the repos/paths it must **not** touch. A layer agent is responsible for its layer only and is explicitly told that the other layers are being built concurrently by other agents against the same contract — so it must code to the contract rather than to what it can currently observe running.\n\n**4. A layer task must be verifiable on its own, against the contract.**\nBecause its counterpart may not exist yet, a layer task's acceptance criteria must be checkable in isolation: the backend task is verified by exercising its endpoints directly (curl/REPL/tests) and matching the contract's payloads; the frontend task is verified against the contract shape (fixtures, mocked client, component tests). Cross-layer integration is verified once, later, by the orchestrator's integration step — not by an individual layer task.\n\n**5. Components form a dependency DAG, tiered for parallelism.**\n- A component with no dependencies is a **root**, tier **A**.\n- Otherwise `tier_index = 1 + max(tier_index of each dependency)` (A=1, B=2, C=3…).\n- Dependencies are between **components**, never between layer tasks of the same component. If you find yourself wanting \"frontend depends on backend\", you have misunderstood this skill: that dependency is exactly what the frozen contract removes.\n- Two components in the same tier must not have conflicting touch-points **within the same repo** — since their same-layer tasks run concurrently in the same repo's worktrees. If they would collide: merge the components, introduce a dependency, or extract the shared surface (shared schema, shared module, migration) into an earlier-tier foundation component.\n\n**6. A real dependency between components is one only the contract cannot dissolve.**\nLegitimate: a later component needs a table, module or shared abstraction that an earlier one physically creates; or the spec's behaviour genuinely layers (feature B operates on data feature A produces). Not legitimate: \"the frontend needs to know the API shape\" — that is contract work, done up front.\n\n## Workflow\n\n### 1. Locate and read the spec\n\nResolve the target spec from the argument (a number like `001`, or a full label). If none given, list `_workflow/specs/spec-*/` and either use the obvious most-recent one or ask which. Read the whole spec — Goals, Non-goals, functional requirements (`FR-N`), acceptance criteria. Every layer task must trace to ≥1 FR; every in-scope FR must be covered.\n\nIf the spec is too vague to pin contracts down, stop and ask. A horizontal decomposition with a loose contract is worse than no decomposition.\n\n### 2. Ground in the real code\n\nGrep/Glob the affected repos (`ardoq-api`, `ardoq-packages`, `devops-monorepo`) to find the actual namespaces, endpoints, tables, components and clients involved, plus a close analogue to mirror for each layer. You cannot write a precise contract from the spec alone — endpoint conventions, serialization casing, auth patterns and generated-client shapes all come from the code. Note per layer: where the code goes, what it must match, and what already exists.\n\n### 3. Cut into components\n\nGroup the spec's functionality into the smallest number of coarse components that respects true dependencies and intra-tier non-collision. Bias toward broad. For each component, list the layers it touches — that determines its layer tasks.\n\nSanity-check each component: *is every cross-layer fact inside it something I can pin down now?* If not, either resolve it or reconsider the boundary.\n\n### 4. Freeze the contract per component\n\nFor each component, write the contract in full (Core Concept 2). Assign each element an owner (producing layer) and its consumers. Then check closure: every element a layer task consumes is produced either by a layer task in the same component, or by an already-completed lower-tier component, or by existing code you have verified exists.\n\n### 5. Derive the layer tasks\n\nFor each component, one task per affected layer. Each is a standalone brief containing the **entire** contract verbatim (not a reference to it — the agent must not have to fetch it), plus that layer's own scope, exclusions, pointers and isolation-testable acceptance criteria.\n\n### 6. Assign tiers and validate\n\nTier the components by the leveling rule, verify acyclicity, run the intra-tier collision check per repo, then run the validation checklist below.\n\n### 7. Write the files and report\n\nWrite the component folders and `index.md` (conventions below). Then report: components per tier, layer tasks per component, the critical path, and any decomposition decisions the user should sanity-check — especially merges, foundation extractions, and any contract element you had to decide yourself rather than read off the spec.\n\n## File & ID conventions\n\nHorizontal output lives in the spec's own folder, under `components/` (so it never collides with a vertical `issues/` decomposition of the same spec):\n\n```\n_workflow/specs/spec-NNN-descriptive-title/\n  spec-NNN-descriptive-title.md\n  components/\n    index.md                                  ← component DAG + tier overview\n    NNN-A1-brief-description/\n      component.md                            ← the frozen contract + shared context\n      NNN-A1-be-brief-description.md          ← backend layer task\n      NNN-A1-fe-brief-description.md          ← frontend layer task\n    NNN-A2-brief-description/\n      component.md\n      NNN-A2-be-brief-description.md\n    NNN-B1-brief-description/\n      ...\n```\n\n**Component ID: `NNN-Xk-brief-description`** — `NNN` spec number (3 digits); `X` tier letter; `k` number within tier; kebab-case slug.\n\n**Layer task ID: `NNN-Xk-<layer>-brief-description`** where `<layer>` is:\n\n| Code | Layer | Repo |\n|---|---|---|\n| `be` | backend (api, service, repo, DB migration) | `ardoq-api` |\n| `fe` | frontend | `ardoq-packages` |\n| `ai` | Python AI services | `devops-monorepo` |\n\nAdd a code only if a new repo genuinely enters scope, and record it in the index. **One layer task per layer per component** — never two tasks in the same repo for the same component (they would collide in the same worktree).\n\nFilenames equal IDs. Example: `004-A1-be-workspace-picker-endpoint.md`.\n\n## Component contract template (`component.md`)\n\n~~~markdown\n# NNN-Xk: <Component Title>\n\n| | |\n|---|---|\n| **Component ID** | NNN-Xk-brief-description |\n| **Spec** | spec-NNN-descriptive-title |\n| **Tier** | X  (parallel with all other tier-X components) |\n| **Depends on** | <component IDs, or `None (root)`> |\n| **Enables** | <component IDs that depend on this> |\n| **Satisfies** | FR-<n>, FR-<m> |\n| **Layer tasks** | NNN-Xk-be-…, NNN-Xk-fe-… (dispatched concurrently) |\n\n## Objective\n\nOne or two sentences: the functionality this component delivers once all its layer tasks are done.\n\n## FROZEN CONTRACT\n\nAuthoritative. Every layer task implements exactly this. No layer may change, rename, extend or \"improve\" any element here; a mismatch found later is resolved by fixing the deviating layer, not by renegotiating the contract. If an element turns out to be genuinely unimplementable, the layer agent stops and escalates rather than improvising a different shape.\n\n### Endpoints\n\n#### `<METHOD> /api/<path>`\n- **Produced by:** NNN-Xk-be-… · **Consumed by:** NNN-Xk-fe-…\n- **Auth / permission:** <e.g. `:allowed?` requires write on workspace>\n- **Path / query params:** <name — type — required? — meaning>\n- **Request body:**\n```json\n{ \"name\": \"string\", \"workspaceId\": \"string\" }\n```\n- **Response 200:**\n```json\n{ \"_id\": \"string\", \"name\": \"string\", \"created\": \"ISO-8601 string\" }\n```\n- **Errors:** `400` <when / body shape>; `403` <when>; `404` <when>\n\n### Field tables\n\n| Field | Type | Required | Meaning | Notes |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| `workspaceId` | string | yes | target workspace | exact casing as written; camelCase on the wire |\n\n### Other boundary-crossing names\n\n| Element | Kind | Exact name | Produced by | Consumed by |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| … | action / flag / column / topic / client symbol | `…` | NNN-Xk-be-… | NNN-Xk-fe-… |\n\n### Empty / edge-case behaviour\n\n- <e.g. absent `foo` ⇒ `null` in the response, not omitted>\n- <e.g. empty list ⇒ `[]` plus `200`, never `404`>\n\n## Shared context\n\nFacts every layer task needs: the spec sections in play, the analogue features to mirror per layer, relevant conventions and gotchas.\n\n## Component acceptance criteria (integration-level)\n\nChecked by the orchestrator's integration step once all layer tasks are merged — not by any single layer task.\n- [ ] <observable end-to-end behaviour, traceable to an FR>\n\n## Layer task summary\n\n| Layer task | Repo | Produces | Consumes |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| NNN-Xk-be-… | ardoq-api | `POST /api/foo` | `foo` table (NNN-A1) |\n| NNN-Xk-fe-… | ardoq-packages | `FooForm` | `POST /api/foo` |\n~~~\n\n## Layer task template\n\nEach layer task is a **standalone brief** — one layer agent executes it with only this file and its repo. It contains the full contract, copied verbatim from `component.md`.\n\n~~~markdown\n# NNN-Xk-<layer>: <Layer Task Title>\n\n| | |\n|---|---|\n| **Task ID** | NNN-Xk-<layer>-brief-description |\n| **Component** | NNN-Xk-brief-description |\n| **Spec** | spec-NNN-descriptive-title |\n| **Layer** | <backend / frontend / AI service> |\n| **Repo** | <ardoq-api / ardoq-packages / devops-monorepo> |\n| **Tier** | X |\n| **Runs in parallel with** | <sibling layer task IDs in this component> |\n| **Satisfies** | FR-<n> |\n\n## Objective\n\nOne or two sentences: what this layer must deliver for the component to work.\n\n## Parallel-work notice\n\nThe other layers of this component are being built **right now** by other agents against the identical contract below. Consequences:\n- Code to the **contract**, not to what you can currently observe running. A counterpart endpoint/component may not exist yet; that is expected and is not a blocker.\n- Do **not** implement, stub into the repo, or \"temporarily\" add anything that belongs to another layer.\n- Do **not** alter any contract element. If one is genuinely unimplementable, stop and escalate — do not substitute a different shape.\n- Stay inside your own repo. Touching another layer's repo will collide with a concurrently running agent.\n\n## FROZEN CONTRACT\n\n<verbatim copy of the component's FROZEN CONTRACT section>\n\n## Inputs — what you receive\n\n- **From the contract:** <elements this layer consumes, with exact names>\n- **From lower-tier components:** <e.g. the `foo` table created by NNN-A1-be-…>\n- **From existing code:** <verified existing helpers/endpoints/components to build on, with paths>\n\n## Outputs — what you must produce\n\nExact, contract-matching deliverables. Another agent is coding against these names right now.\n- <e.g. `POST /api/foo` handler with `:allowed?`, returning the Response 200 body exactly as specified>\n- <e.g. `foo-service/create!` — signature: …>\n\n## Scope — in\n\nChecklist for this layer only.\n- [ ] <…>\n- [ ] <tests proving this layer satisfies the contract in isolation>\n\n## Scope — out (do NOT touch)\n\n- <the sibling layers' repos, named explicitly>\n- <files owned by other same-tier components in this repo>\n\n## Context & pointers\n\n- Analogue to mirror: <path to a similar existing implementation in this repo>\n- <conventions, constraints, gotchas>\n\n## Acceptance criteria (verifiable without the other layers)\n\n- [ ] <e.g. `curl POST /api/foo` with the contract request returns the contract 200 body, field-for-field>\n- [ ] <e.g. `FooForm` renders and submits the contract request shape against a mocked client>\n\n## Definition of done\n\n- [ ] Every in-scope item implemented in this repo only\n- [ ] Every contract element this layer produces matches the contract exactly — names, casing, types, status codes\n- [ ] Tests written and passing\n- [ ] Nothing belonging to another layer added\n- [ ] Conforms to repo conventions\n~~~\n\n## Index template (`components/index.md`)\n\n~~~markdown\n# spec-NNN — Component graph (horizontal)\n\nGenerated from spec-NNN-descriptive-title. Tiers execute in order. Within a tier, all components run in parallel; within a component, all layer tasks run in parallel against a frozen contract.\n\n## Dependency graph\n\n```mermaid\ngraph TD\n  A1[A1 short-name]\n  A2[A2 short-name]\n  B1[B1 short-name]\n  A1 --> B1\n  A2 --> B1\n```\n\n## Tiers\n\n### Tier A — no dependencies (start here, all in parallel)\n| Component | Title | Layer tasks | Satisfies | Repos |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| NNN-A1-… | … | be, fe | FR-1, FR-2 | ardoq-api, ardoq-packages |\n\n### Tier B — depends on A\n| Component | Title | Depends on | Layer tasks | Satisfies | Repos |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| NNN-B1-… | … | A1, A2 | be, fe, ai | FR-3 | … |\n\n## Layer codes in use\n| Code | Layer | Repo |\n|---|---|---|\n| be | backend | ardoq-api |\n| fe | frontend | ardoq-packages |\n\n## Coverage\nEvery in-scope FR maps to ≥1 layer task: FR-1 → A1-be, A1-fe; FR-2 → …\n\n## Contract index\nOne line per boundary-crossing element, so collisions across components are visible at a glance.\n| Element | Component | Produced by | Consumed by |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| `POST /api/foo` | NNN-A1 | A1-be | A1-fe |\n\n## Critical path\nA1 → B1 → C2  (longest component chain — the minimum number of sequential waves)\n~~~\n\n## Validation checklist (run before reporting)\n\n- **Acyclic**: no cycles among components.\n- **Tier correctness**: `tier = 1 + max(dependency tiers)`; roots are tier A.\n- **No intra-tier component dependencies**.\n- **No layer-task dependencies**: no layer task of a component depends on a sibling layer task. If one does, the contract is incomplete — finish it.\n- **One task per repo per component**: no two layer tasks of one component share a repo.\n- **No intra-tier repo collisions**: same-tier components' tasks in the same repo don't touch the same files/endpoints/migrations conflictingly.\n- **Contract completeness**: every endpoint has method, path, params, request body, response body, status codes and error cases; every wire field has a name, type, requiredness and meaning; every boundary-crossing name is literal, not descriptive.\n- **Contract closure**: every element a task consumes is produced by a sibling task, a lower-tier component, or verified existing code.\n- **Contract embedded**: every layer task file carries the full contract verbatim, not a pointer.\n- **Isolation-testability**: every layer task's acceptance criteria are checkable without its sibling layers existing.\n- **FR coverage**: every in-scope FR covered by ≥1 layer task; every layer task traces to ≥1 FR.\n\n## Rules\n\n- Slice by **component, then by layer**. Every layer of a component runs concurrently; no layer waits on another.\n- **Freeze the contract before writing any layer task.** Exact names, exact casing, exact types, exact status codes. Never a description where a literal belongs.\n- **Embed the contract verbatim** in every layer task. A layer agent must never have to go find it.\n- **One layer task per repo per component.** Two agents in one repo for one component is a collision, not parallelism.\n- Component dependencies only where physically real. \"Needs to know the API shape\" is not a dependency — it is contract work.\n- Prefer **fewer, broader components**; the per-agent load is already divided by layer.\n- Do not implement anything — this skill produces the decomposition only.\n- If contract elements can't be pinned down from the spec plus the code, resolve them or ask the user.
